### Page 1

CPI 公布后，交易员开始预计 12 ⽉有较⼩可能加息 50 个基点；美联储今年票委洛根称，今
冬可能需要更快加息，联储⾄少还需两次 25 基点加息再暂停。此后，美债收益率升幅进⼀
步扩⼤， 10 年期收益率重上 5.0% 、⽇内升超 10 个基点；现货⻩⾦⼀度跌超 3% 、现货⽩银
曾跌超 4% 。
摘要：
美国 10 ⽉通胀再度超出市场预期，美债遭遇抛售，收益率全线⼤幅攀升；美元⾛强、利率上⾏
预期升温，也令⻩⾦和⽩银承压。美联储官员接连释放鹰派信号，市场不仅进⼀步押注 12 ⽉加
息，也开始评估更⼤幅度加息以及明年进⼀步收紧货币政策的可能性。
美东时间 10 ⽇周⼆，美国 CPI 公布后，美国国债遭集中抛售，美债价格跳⽔、收益率加速上⾏。
基准 10 年期美债收益率在 CPI 公布后升幅扩⼤，美联储官员洛根（ Lorie Logan ）讲话后，美股
午盘时段⼀度升破 5.01% ，⽇内升超 10 个基点，对美联储政策利率预期更敏感的 2 年期美债收益
率升幅更⼤，美股午盘曾升破 4.77% ，⽇内升超 20 个基点，势将创去年 4 ⽉以来最⼤单⽇升幅。

贵⾦属市场同步承压。美国 CPI 公布后，⻩⾦盘中跌幅扩⼤。美股午盘时段，纽约⻩⾦期货刷新
⽇低⾄ 4020.8 美元 / 盎司，⽇内跌近 2.3% ；现货⻩⾦曾跌⾄ 4014 美元 / 盎司下⽅，⽇内跌近
3.1% 。⽩银跌势更为明显，⾏情显⽰，美股午盘刷新⽇低时，纽约期银⽇内跌近 3% ，现货⽩银
⽇内跌超 4% 。

### Page 2

CPI 通胀连⽉超预期，债市重新定价美联储政策路径
美国劳⼯统计局周⼆公布的数据显⽰， 10 ⽉ CPI 环⽐上涨 0.4% ，⾼于市场预期的 0.3% ，但较 9 ⽉
的 0.6% 有所放缓；剔除⻝品和能源价格的核⼼ CPI 环⽐上涨 0.3% ，同样⾼于市场预期的 0.2% ，
与 9 ⽉涨幅持平。这也是核⼼ CPI 连续第三个⽉上涨 0.3% 。由于去年同期美国联邦政府停摆导致
相关数据⽆法采集，劳⼯统计局此次没有公布 10 ⽉ CPI 同⽐数据。
CPI 数据公布⼏⼩时前，市场已经受到美联储理事沃勒（ Christopher Waller ）的鹰派⾔论冲
击。
沃勒本周⼆在伦敦发表讲话时表⽰，即使 10 ⽉通胀有所改善，他仍倾向于⽀持美联储 12 ⽉加息
25 个基点；但如果核⼼ CPI 连续第三个⽉达到 0.3% 或更⾼，就应考虑在 12 ⽉加息 50 个基点。他
警告称，只加息 25 个基点可能损害美联储抗通胀的公信⼒。
实际公布的核⼼ CPI 恰好达到沃勒设定的 0.3% ⻔槛。数据公布后，市场迅速提⾼对更⼤幅度加

息的押注。路透报道称，交易员对美联储 12 ⽉加息 50 个基点的隐含概率⼀度升⾄约 13.6% ，⽽
数据公布前这⼀概率接近于零；相⽐之下， 25 个基点加息已基本成为市场主流预期。
彭博报道称，通胀数据推动美债遭遇抛售，短期限美债承受的压⼒尤其明显。货币市场也开始
加⼤对美联储到 2027 年年中⾄少再加息两次的押注，并更明显地计⼊ 12 ⽉⼀次性加息 50 个基点
的可能性。
Principal Asset Management ⾸席全球策略师 Seema Shah 表⽰： “ 周⼆的通胀报告是⼀记警
告。它向美联储传递的信息令⼈不安但⾮常明确，那就是和通胀的战⽃还没有结束。 ”
⾼盛资产管理多部⻔固定收益投资主管 Lindsay Rosner 表⽰，如果 CPI 持续强劲，美联储可能推
动更激进的紧缩周期。她的观点反映出市场对通胀持续⾼企的担忧：即便 12 ⽉加息已经成为基
准预期，后续政策路径仍可能⽐此前预想的更加鹰派。
今年票委洛根：今冬或需更快加息，联储⾄少还需两次 25 基点加息再暂停

CPI 公布后，达拉斯联储主席洛根也释放了进⼀步收紧货币政策的信号。洛根是今年拥有 FOMC
会议投票权的票委。她周⼆在达拉斯联储和堪萨斯城联储联合举办的能源会议上表⽰，按照当
前经济和通胀前景，美联储⾄少还需要实施两次 25 个基点的加息。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

洛根指出，具体加息节奏取决于短期经济和⾦融环境，今年冬季美联储有可能⽐今年秋季采取
更快的加息步伐。她表⽰： “ 今年冬季可能有必要⽐ FOMC 今年秋季迄今采取的步伐更快地⾏
动。 ”
不过，洛根并⾮主张持续不断地加息。她表⽰，在完成上述两次加息后，美联储可以暂停⾏
动，评估经济和⾦融市场的发展，再决定是否需要进⼀步调整政策。她还强调，通胀⻓期⾼于
⽬标，可能逐渐嵌⼊企业和家庭的⾏为预期，因此及时采取⾏动⼗分重要。
洛根的表态意味着，部分美联储官员正在讨论的不仅是 12 ⽉是否加息，还包括如何更快地将利
率推向温和限制性⽔平。她与沃勒的讲话前后呼应，令市场对美联储未来数⽉进⼀步收紧政策
的担忧升温。
美债收益率和美元⻬升，⾦银遭双重压⼒
美债收益率和美元同步⾛强，给不⽣息的贵⾦属带来压⼒。⼀⽅⾯，债券收益率上升提⾼了持

有⻩⾦、⽩银等⾮⽣息资产的机会成本；另⼀⽅⾯，美元⾛强也令以美元计价的贵⾦属对其他
货币持有者更加昂贵。
路透报道称， 10 ⽉通胀数据⾼于预期后，⻩⾦价格明显下跌，市场开始评估美联储采取更激进
加息⾏动的可能性。⻩⾦对利率预期变化较为敏感，若市场进⼀步上调对未来政策利率的预
期，贵⾦属短期仍可能⾯临压⼒。
不过，⻩⾦和⽩银的后续⾛势并不只取决于美联储政策。美元⾛势、地缘政治⻛险、能源价格
以及投资者对经济前景的判断，都可能影响贵⾦属需求。当前市场的关键变化在于，通胀数据
和美联储官员的鹰派表态重新强化了利率上⾏预期，使此前受益于降息预期的贵⾦属交易⾯临
调整。

Document 341: EcoNews of 9 November 2026 (2026-11-09)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 9 November 2026 (2026-11-09).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-09
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.41 / 9 November 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
In brief. The week was dominated by large market swings triggered by
the US midterms results. In the end, energy prices end the week much
lower, bond yields somewhat lower and stock prices down in tech-heavy
markets and slightly up elsewhere, notably in Europe. Incoming data
confirmed a picture of resilient growth overall. The Bank of England

hiked its policy rate by 25 bps and signaled it could do more. Market
pressure on France eased somewhat.
EUROZONE
Energy pushes inflation up, but underlying pressures remain rela -
tively contained. Headline inflation rose to 4.0% y/y in October, its
highest in three years, strengthening the case for another ECB rate
increase in December. Energy remained the main driver, while core in-
flation rose by 2.6%. The ECB wage tracker points to negotiated wage

growth slowing to 2.3% by September 2027. Meanwhile, retail sales fell
0.7% m/m in September and the average interest rate on new business
loans increased 13bp to 3.90%.
To watch next week: September trade balance, Q3 GDP second esti -
mate and employment (Friday).
France
EVs boost activity, market pressure eases somewhat. After already
bolstering household consumption in Q3, new car registrations rose
7% y/y in October (+4% y/y YTD), driven by another surge in electric

car sales (+81% y/y in October; +78% y/y YTD). In parallel, manufac -
turing output rebounded by 0.5% m/m in September, supported by
the automotive sector (+7.5% m/m but -5.5% y/y) and capital goods
(+2.6% m/m and +4.8% y/y). However, industrial production fell by
0.5% m/m (and 1.3% y/y), as a result of a decline in electricity produc-
tion (-4.2% m/m). The composite October PMI was revised down by
1.8 points from the flash estimate to 46.6 (-4.5 m/m), dragged down by

services. This sharp downgrade likely reflects the hit to sentiment from
the October 17 riots; however, precedents suggest activity itself should
not be visibly impacted. Appropriately indifferent to the meandering
journey of the draft budget through Parliament, the OAT-bund spread
narrowed to 155 bps (-10 bps in one week) amid a broad-based decline
in government bond yields across advanced economies; Thursday’s
long-term debt auction, the last of the year, was well-subscribed. Stri-

ving to meet its deficit target of 5.4% of GDP in 2026, the government
announced EUR3.8bn of spending cuts to offset EUR3.7bn in additional
expenditure. The High Council for Public Finance nevertheless sees the
deficit reaching 5.5%. The 2027 budget will start being discussed by
the Senate from next week, in its original version, after the national
Assembly unanimously rejected its amended version that would have
led to a 5.9% deficit.
To watch next week: Q3 unemployment rate and the Banque de France

monthly business survey (Tuesday).
Germany
Industrial activity picks up. New orders rebounded 4.4% m/m in Sep -
tember and industrial production increased 0.7%, with the auto sec -
tor delivering particularly striking gains of 10.5% in orders and 17.1%
in production. Mechanical-engineering orders also recovered (+8.3%),
while pharmaceutical production increased by 8.0%. By contrast, large
orders in aerospace, shipbuilding, rail and defence fell by 10.4%, while

machinery and equipment production declined by 6.5%. October's com-
posite PMI was revised up to 53.4, signaling continued strong expan -
sion.
Italy
Services keep the economy above water as manufacturing slips back.
The composite PMI eased to 50.8 in October, its lowest since May.
Manufacturing returned to contraction at 49.4, its weakest reading
since January, while services strengthened to 52.0 and recorded a fifth
consecutive month of growth. Both sectors reported increasing input-

cost and output-price inflation.
UNITED KINGDOM
The BoE hikes and leaves the door open to more. The Bank of England
raised Bank Rate by 25bp to 4%, its first increase since August 2023, in a
more hawkish than expected 8-1 vote, with Catherine Mann favouring
a 50bp hike. The November Monetary Policy Report sees CPI inflation
peaking around 5% y/y by mid-2027 and upgraded Q4 2026 growth to
2.2%, while cutting the 2027 and 2028 growth projections. The com -

posite PMI meanwhile improved to 52.9, its highest since February,
with new business growing at its strongest pace in nine months and
private-sector employment increasing for the first time in 25 months.
To watch next week: Q3 GDP , business investment, industrial produc-
tion (Thursday).
UNITED STATES
Post election market jitters and a hot jobs report. The Democrats’
unexpectedly sweeping victory in the midterms (the largest wave
since 1974) initially led markets to rally on (i) relief that the President

recognised the results, and (ii) hopes of a swift end to the Iran war
(oil prices and bond yields down, stock prices up). But this was soon
followed by a sharp equity sell-off on 5 November, with the S&P 500
down 4.4% and the Nasdaq down 5.0%, erasing USD2.7 Trillion of mar-
ket capitalization in one day. Why? Because of the realization that vo -
ters strongly supported AI-restrictive measures on the ballot (46 out
of 47) and candidates — for Congress and Governor seats — in favour

of curtailing data centers buildout. By the week-end, however, senti -
ment had turned around again, helped in part by a much stronger than
expected jobs report: payrolls increased by 165,000 in October against
a 70,000 consensus, while the previous two months were revised hi -
gher and unemployment edged down to 4.1%. Average hourly earnings
rose 0.4% m/m, their strongest monthly pace since November 2025.
Manufacturing activity also strengthened, with the ISM rising to 55.5,

and the non-manufacturing index easing to a still buoyant 54.5.
To watch next week: October CPI (Tuesday); October PPI (Friday).
JAPAN
Wage growth accelerates. Scheduled contractual earnings increased
3.7% y/y in September and total wage growth accelerated to 4.6%,
helped by strong bonus payments. Real wage growth remained posi -
tive at 2.2% y/y.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Portfolio outflows. According to IIF data, non-resident portfolio invest-
ment flows recorded USD33.8bn of outflows in October, the largest

monthly outflow since March and up from USD19.3bn in September.
Equities accounted for USD31.7bn, led by USD28.3bn from emerging
Asia, as doubts over AI-investment returns and profit-taking hit North
Asian markets particularly hard. Taiwan and South Korea suffered a
further sharp market correction in the first week of November.

### Page 2

7
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.41 / 9 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ASIA
PMI: expanding except for the Philippines. The ASEAN PMI rose to 53.1
in October from 52.1, pointing to faster expansion. India remained ro -
bust despite a slight decline (54.7). In Taiwan and South Korea, the
manufacturing PMIs declined but remained expansionary, at 54.9 and
51.3, respectively. Indonesia’s real GDP growth accelerated to 5.5% y/y

in Q3 2026 from 5.3% in Q2 marking its fastest pace since 2013.
China
The PBOC steps in as equity correction deepens. The central bank in -
creased its use of liquidity tools designed to support equity purchases,
including the special relending facility for share buybacks and stake in-
creases and a third round of the Securities, Funds and Insurance Com-
panies Swap Facility, for which 31 institutions applied for more than
RMB100bn. The measures follow heavy stock-market losses between

June and October. The PBOC also raised the reserve-requirement ratio
on foreign-currency deposits to 6% from 4%, effective 15 November, in
an effort to slow the renminbi's appreciation.
Weak domestic inflation pressure: In October, CPI inflation slowed to
0.7% y/y from 0.9% in September. Fuel prices increased by 20.2% y/y,
food prices fell -2.9% and core inflation slowed to 0.8% y/y from 1.0%.
Producer price inflation continued to rise (reaching 5.7% vs. 5.0% in

September), principally driven by higher energy and electronics prices.
To watch next week: External-trade data (Tuesday).
EMEA
Central Europe
PMIs expansionary except in Romania. Poland’s PMI hit the 50-mark
for the first time since April 2025 thanks to export orders. PMI was
also higher in Czech Rep. (55), supported by new orders, output and
employment. Hungary’s PMI fell marginally to 53.1.
Poland and Czech Republic central banks hold and hike respectively,

as expected.
Gulf
PMIs resilient except Qatar (deteriorating further to 46.3).
Saudi Arabia: Massive budget deficit in sight. Saudi Arabia recorded a
budget deficit of USD22.5bn in Q3, narrowing by 5% y/y. But over the
first nine months of 2026, budget deficit reached USD65.1bn, up from
USD48bn in 2025, despite a rather good revenue performance (+2.8%
total; +7.8% for non-oil revenues; -1.3% for oil revenues). The 8% rise in
spending in Q1-Q3 partly due to the war will make it difficult to meet

the 2026 revised target (deficit of 4.9% of GDP).
LATIN AMERICA
PMIs remain weak across the region, with only Colombia in expansion.
Brazil’s central bank pauses rate cuts, whereas Mexico’s central bank
cuts rates whilst signalling an upcoming pause. On 4 November, Bra -
zil’s central bank held its Selic rate at 13.75%, interrupting a series of
five consecutive cuts, amid rising inflation (4.6% y/y in September, up
from 4.2%), a sharp post-election real depreciation on fiscal concerns,

and higher energy prices. The central bank also revised up its 2027
inflation forecast to 4.7% (from 3.9%) and removed forward guidance
hinting at further cuts. Mexico’s central bank unexpectedly cut its po -
licy rate by 25bp to 6.25% on 5 November. This decision came despite
above-target inflation (3.5% against a 3% target), a peso depreciation
of more than 9% against USD since September, and relatively strong Q3
GDP growth (3.2% y/y). The central bank signaled that rates would now

remain on hold for an extended period.
COMMODITIES
Geopolitical relief delivers a dramatic energy-price reversal. Mar -
kets have priced in the reduced risk of short-term conflict escalation
through a sharp decline in prices. Beyond this, however, they lack di -
rection, wavering between positive signals (the resumption of indirect
negotiations between the belligerents) and less favorable ones (in -
creased vessel damage in the strait). Brent dropped 7.6% over the week

to USD96.8/bbl as markets priced a reduced risk of near-term conflict
escalation. European gas prices fell almost in lockstep, declining 7.7%
to EUR87.6/MWh. US commercial crude inventories continued to de -
cline, although more modestly than over the preceding four weeks.
Diesel crack spread’s reaction to US elections has been much more
limited than for crude. EU gas storage ended the refilling season at 75%
of capacity (vs. 80% goal).
y/y = year-on-year

m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels
MBtu = Million British Thermal Units

Document 347: Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports (2026-11-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports (2026-11-02).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 16
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports
Europe’s auto deal with China is a good start. But containing China’s export boom
will require a much broader response.
European Commissioner for Trade and Economic Security Maros Sefcovic speaks at a press briefing at the EU Delegation to
China in Beijing, China October 9, 2026. Florence Lo/Reuters
PUBLISHED
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
November 2, 2026 11:02 a.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The year-over-year increase in Chinese automobile
exports to all markets is currently about 3.5 million
cars.
Over ten years, that is 35 million cars. The global
market excluding China is only around 60 million
cars. Japan raised its auto exports by about 3.5 million
cars in the 1970s—but that was over a decade, not
one year. And right now the U.S. and Indian markets

(around 20 million cars) are effectively closed to
Chinese production. The 2026 jump in exports is an
incredible.
China’s domestic market, though, is down 20 percent
this year—that works out to a decline of about 5
million cars. The Wall Street Journal estimates that
domestic sales will fall to around 19 million cars this
year, less than a third of the production capacity of
China’s auto industry.
So even though China’s exports are increasing at an

unprecedented pace, China’s domestic auto sector
isn’t really booming; production is essentially flat, and
intense domestic competition is destroying the
profitability of firms that don’t export.*

### Page 3

3
Europe’s new auto deal with China should be
understood in this context.
The deal limits China’s exports of hybrids (including
plug-in hybrids) to Europe to around 350,000 cars.
That is above the 300,000 hybrids that China
exported to Europe in 2025, but well below the (gulp)
800,000 annualized pace for hybrids in the last three
months of (European) data. Total imports,
annualized, were running at an incredible 2 million a
year pace thanks to strong battery electric imports in

September.

### Page 4

4
China appears to have concluded that it prefers a
guaranteed market for a limited number of cars (and
more or less guaranteed profits) to the risk of a
prohibitive tariff that would cut off all its exports.
And Europe preferred a negotiated deal to the risk of
retaliation and a slowdown in licensing rare earth and
magnet exports.
The Commission also thinks of this as a bit of a
model—more on that later.
I certainly understand the logic of the deal.

Something needed to be done. China’s export of
hybrids to Europe were up an insane 200 percent
year-over-year in the last 3 months of data, which led
to a 75 percent increase for all cars. One more year of
75 percent growth would bring China’s exports to

### Page 5

5
around 3 million cars; in two years, close to 6 million,
and in three years, 10 million—or most of the market.
But Europe still is a long way from a coherent trade
and industrial policy with respect to autos, let alone a
coherent economic policy towards overall trade with
China.
After this deal, as I understand it:
Hybrid imports are capped at 350,000, down
from a projected 800,000 in 2026—and then
rising to 370,000 and 390,000 in subsequent
years.

Battery electric vehicles face a tariff from 25
percent (Telsa, 10 percent base + 15 percent) to
45 percent (10 percent base plus 35 percent).
BYD’s total tariff is at 27 percent, which isn’t
prohibitive. Even with these tariffs, battery
electric imports will total around 600,000 this
year, up from 500,000 last year.
Diesel and gasoline (ICE) cars face a tariff of
10 percent. Europe is importing around
200,000 of these a year (including a significant

number from Chinese-owned MG).
There are no special tariffs on imported parts or
imported batteries, which matters for the growing
number of Chinese factories in Europe. So long as
tariffs are low and the renminbi is undervalued,
Chinese firms still have an incentive to maximize the

### Page 6

6
Chinese content of their “transplant” factories (ask
Thailand).
There is currently no unified European policy on
inward investment that requires joint ventures or
technology transfer either, only a lot of wishful
thinking.**
Sum it up, and it is a market where Chinese exports
are constrained by a hodge-podge of different trade
defense tools, and there is no standard policy for
ensuring that Chinese assembly operations use
European parts rather than import Chinese kits. The

existing operations, which are just starting to scale up,
don’t draw on a European supply chain. They likely
preserve a few assembly jobs at the expense of the
broader automotive ecosystem.
A coherent European (or national) industrial policy
for the automotive and battery sectors is thus still a
work in progress.
The safeguard on hybrids and the resulting tariff rate
quota only stops the bleeding.
And make no mistake, Europe’s position in the global

automotive sector is slipping. Europe still runs an
overall surplus in auto trade, but that surplus is falling
fast if trade in batteries is added to automotive trade
(there isn’t a split between batteries used for energy

### Page 7

7
storage and batteries used by cars in the EU data
right now, at least not that I have found).
Europe’s broader trade policy to China is similarly a
work in progress.
The surge in imports of hybrid cars was particularly
pronounced, but it is by no means the only such surge.
The balance in industrial machinery has eroded
significantly. Consider the following chart, which
shows imports and exports of machinery, electronics
and vehicles (HS 84, 85, and 87) by weight.

### Page 8

8
The balance in chemicals, measured by weight to
abstract from price changes, shows the same trend.
Europe doesn’t have an across-the-board tool for
applying tariffs against China like the U.S. section
301 statute.

### Page 9

9
But that isn’t necessarily an immediate constraint, as
there are plenty of solid safeguard cases thanks to the
current surge in European imports from China.
But if each safeguard needs to include a negotiated
tariff-rate quota that gives China enough guaranteed
market access (and Chinese firms enough guaranteed
profits) to ensure trade peace, Europe needs to get
moving with negotiation in other sectors.
Chemicals and machinery are more diverse sectors

than cars and thus aren’t as amenable to the same
high-level Š ef č ovi č -Wang negotiations the led to the
most recent deal.
This critique isn’t meant as criticism. Europe needed
to act on cars. Look at a chart of automotive trade in
kilos.

### Page 10

10
It’s just that it also needs to act in other sectors: auto
parts, batteries (though that is complicated),
chemicals, and some categories of industrial
machinery. The scale of China’s export boom and the
fact that in most sectors there are no limits to China’s
ability to scale up is a real problem.***
And Europe cannot wait for China to conclude that
it would rather grow on the basis of internal, rather
than external, demand.
I disagree with the IMF’s former top brass on this. A

China that grows on the back of a 1.5 pp net export
contribution for another couple of years will
deindustrialize much of the world. China’s trading
partners cannot step aside and do nothing until
China figures out how to escape deflation using its
own demand. Xi appears more than happy to
continue with his current policy mix and gamble that

### Page 11

11
the world will continue to absorb China’s ever
growing export surplus.
Pascal Lamy, Nicolas Köhler-Suzuki, and Victor Luiz
do Prado of the Jacques Delors Institute recognize
the need for a more systemic response—as do Jean-
Pierre Landau and Sébastien Jean, who have done an
excellent job explaining how China’s micro- and
macro-industrial policies fit together into a coherent,
self-reinforcing whole.
The proposals in the Lamy et al. paper overlap heavily

with the Commission’s current approach to China.
The authors envision a two-pronged approach: first,
negotiating with China to seek agreement on tariff-
rate quotas that effectively limit the EU’s imports
from China; and second, developing a broad yet
targeted tariff-based instrument to address structural
imbalances should negotiations fail. The latter would
give Europe a credible threat should China refuse to
agree to the proposed safeguards.
My own view: don’t let the perfect be the enemy of

the good.
The EU has shied away from developing its own 301
tool out of concern about preserving a “rules-based
system.” I still think a European 301 would allow a
more fulsome response, but understand that
European isn’t as willing as the United States to use

### Page 12

12
tools that at least appear to be outside those
authorized by the WTO. In reality the EU no longer
really wants to trade with China on the same (MFN)
terms applied to other WTO members. That mirrors
the current view of the United States. But the EU is
willing to go through more procedural hoops to get to
a similar result.****
But I also recognize that, absent policy adjustment in
China—notably a stronger currency that allows for
fair market competition—the scope of safeguards and

managed trade will progressively expand.
* One sign: Stellantis is resuming Jeep production in
China, but primarily for export rather than the
domestic market.
** There is a lot of (in my view) somewhat confused
analysis of Chinese investment that works off the
analogy provided by either foreign investment in
China’s auto sector or Japanese investment in the U.S.
and Europe in the 1980s. These are relevant examples
of course, but asserting that Chinese investment will

result in a similar outcome when the underlying
economics differ is a big logical jump. Japanese firms
had incentives to localize their parts production in the
United States from the then-strong yen as well as
from the risk of protection. Foreign firms operating in
China had to do JVs until recently and had incentives
to localize from China’s significant tariff on auto

### Page 13

13
parts and China’s lower cost structure (in part because
of a weak RMB). Tesla’s Shanghai incentives and tax
deal included strong localization incentives, and there
were additional incentives from Tesla’s desire to
qualify for the lists the governed access to China’s
consumer EV subsidies. That is why something like
90 percent of Tesla parts, including its batteries, are
now made in China. Chinese investors in Europe
have no similar incentives to localize right now, yet

many assume that the results will be the same. That,
to me, is naïve. Since the underlying economics differ
(Europe’s cost basis is much higher than existing
production in China) getting Chinese firms to do
more than final assembly in Europe will take stronger
policy tools than were required to get foreign firms to
localize parts production in China. Europe’s
Industrial Accelerator Act would be a step in the
right direction, but more is likely needed. Thailand

provides a useful cautionary example.
*** China can make over 60 million cars, according to
recent reporting from the Wall Street Journal. The
domestic market this year will be under 20 million
cars (down from something like 24 million cars last
year). Even with exports of 10 million, there is ~30
million cars worth of unused auto production
capacity (roughly double European demand). And
China is still investing at a pace that is consistent
with adding close to 5 million of EV production

capacity a year. Chinese firms, who don’t make any
money on domestic sales, are strongly incentivized to

### Page 14

14

From Follow the Money and Greenberg Center for Geoeconomics
export. The world has never encountered such elastic
supply.
**** The more different the legal basis for the
European and U.S. response—and the more policy
differences (TRQs vs. a flat tariff, for example) the

harder it would be to ever develop a harmonized
policy approach. But with President Trump, a
harmonized policy isn’t even a U.S. goal.

Global Views: Downside Growth Risks
26 October 2026 | 6:45AM EDT
1. So far in 2026, global growth has been remarkably unremarkable.  Both the world 
as a whole and the G3 economies are growing in line with our estimates of potential 
GDP .  Our interpretation is that fiscal easing and the positive impulse from the AI 
boom have offset the negative impulse from the war in Iran and the lingering drag 
from higher US tariffs.

2. Our standing global GDP forecasts project more of the same in 2027, as lower 
energy prices offset fading tailwinds from fiscal policy and AI.  But the balance of 
risks is on the downside.  The most immediate concern is that, far from subsiding, 
energy prices have again climbed sharply on fears of renewed Middle East escalation.  
The prices of gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, and European/Asian natural gas are now near 
or even above their March/April highs, with likely negative effects on consumers and 
energy-importing economies more broadly.

3. Although we don’t expect significant fiscal policy changes in 2027, the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—have 
started to show up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates.  The moves have 
been mostly orderly, but the increase since early 2026 averages a sizable 100bp across 
DM yield curves.  If sustained, we estimate that this increase will subtract a cumulative 
0.6% from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment.  
(Only part of the drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect bond 
yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.)

Exhibit 2: Oil Price Risks Are Skewed to the Upside

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

### Page 3

4. The growth impulse from the AI boom was set to peak in late 2026 or early 2027 even 
before concerns around the sustainability of the AI investment boom intensified in 
recent weeks.  Although AI investment is still rising rapidly, we expect the increase as a 
share of GDP to slow over the next year.  Alongside the fading boost from last year’s 
increase in US depreciation allowances, this will weigh on capital spending growth, 
especially in the US.  AI has also boosted US consumer spending growth via a positive 
wealth effect worth an estimated 0.4pp (or 0.25% of GDP) over the past year.  This 
impact is likely to decline over the next year, gradually if stocks perform in line with our 
strategists’ reasonably upbeat expectations but more sharply if AI-related stocks 
continue to decline.

5. Our own base case remains that AI investment will ultimately pay off in terms of higher 
productivity and economywide profits.  However, one key channel for this payoff is labor 
market disruption, which has negative near-term macroeconomic effects.  And while DM 
labor markets still look solid in aggregate, we are starting to see clearer signs that the AI 
impact is spreading beyond the technology sector.  Across nonmanufacturing industries, 
the share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment.

6. The Fed’s recent signals—explicit from Williams/Jefferson and implicit from Warsh—
effectively rule out a hike on October 28.  But the FOMC still looks very likely to deliver 
another 25bp hike to 4-4.25% on December 9 because spot inflation remains too high.  
And while we continue to expect significant disinflation next year on the back of 
reduced impulses from tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related prices, the risks to our 
forecast are on the upside, most obviously in energy.  Higher inflation could result in

7. Like the Fed, the ECB will likely hold rates steady next week and deliver a final hike (to 
2.75%) at the December meeting.  However, the turbulence in the French bond market 
poses somewhat greater downside risks to that call than in the US.  The turbulence has 
tightened financial conditions to a degree that is likely to shave ¼-½pp from annualized 
growth in coming quarters.  And if it intensifies into December, markets might perceive a 
hike as signaling insufficient focus by the ECB on the risk of Euro area fragmentation.  
While the ECB could address this issue by coupling a hike with renewed balance sheet 
expansion, the hurdle for such a move is high, in part because purchases of French 
bonds would inevitably raise concerns about moral hazard with respect to the needed 
fiscal adjustment.

### Page 6

November 5.  Consistent with our view that the UK economy is stronger than widely 
perceived, GDP growth has surprised on the upside this year and the unemployment rate 
declined to 4.7% in August on a 3-month average basis (with the volatile 1-month rate 
plunging to 4.1%).  Rising inflation and the looming household energy price reset in 
January imply a significant risk of further moves, although market pricing of 4+ hikes 
(including November) still looks excessive.  We also expect hikes in Canada and New 
Zealand this week and in Australia next week.

9. Slightly faster than expected growth in Q3 has pushed up our 2026 GDP forecast for 
China to 4.6%.  But most activity indicators are much weaker.  Both property activity and 
fixed asset investment are contracting year-on-year, and while nominal retail sales 
growth picked up to +1.1% in September, large retailers (whose sales are relatively 
better measured) are still posting substantial year-on-year declines.  With domestic 
demand rising slowly at best, sustained GDP growth in the official 4.5-5% target range 
will require ongoing steep increases in the trade surplus.  But the surplus is already at 1% 
of global GDP , and further increases will eat more noticeably into output and 
employment abroad.  Ultimately, China’s trading partners may respond by imposing 
trade barriers that weigh on Chinese exports and GDP , although the measures taken so 
far have been limited.

Exhibit 8: UK Growth Forecasts Have Drifted Higher

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 7

10. Our strongest global view is that central banks will deliver fewer hikes than currently 
priced.  In addition, our rates strategists flag underweight positioning, diminished 
technical risks, and risk asset pressures as reasons why the recent rally might extend.  
The market’s narrow breadth makes our equity strategists wary of momentum factor 
exposure, but they still expect the S&P 500 to resume its climb after the midterm 
election, as earnings continue to grow solidly.  Our credit strategists remain cautious on 
IG technology bonds but see scope for some near-term reversal in HY given the scale of 
the recent selloff.  Our FX strategists expect the Japanese yen to act as a hedge against 
further AI jitters, in part because such jitters would shrink US-Japan interest rate 
differentials.  And our commodity strategists recommend targeted exposures to oil 
products and European natural gas to hedge near-term escalation risks alongside 
longer-term exposures to copper, gold, and select power markets to benefit from 
structural supply/demand imbalances.

Document 370: India_ A middle class too small to supplant China’s economic role (2026-10-26)

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
At a time when Chinese consumption is slowing significantly, could India become a driver of global consumption? For now, this
prospect presents considerable difficulties, with many obstacles to overcome. Despite dynamic growth and the Indian economy’s
expanding share of global output (8.2% of worldwide GDP in 2025), the middle-class consumer population remains modest and is nowhere
near the size of China’s.
This is due to the concentration of employment in low-value-added and largely informal sectors. The few manufacturing sectors with

high value-added and strong job potential — electrical equipment, electronics, automotive, chemicals and pharmaceuticals — continue to
face constraints from an unfavourable business environment and a skills shortage that is holding back their move into premium market
segments.
Furthermore, the highest-paying service sector jobs, particularly in information technology, are now threatened by the rise of
artificial intelligence. Without concrete measures, the “demographic window” — which is expected to remain favourable for another 15 to

25 years — could close before India establishes a solid industrial base and a sufficiently large middle class.
INDIA: A MIDDLE CLASS TOO SMALL TO SUPPLANT CHINA’S ECONOMIC ROLE
Johanna Melka
issue 20
26 October 2026

### Page 2

The bank
for a changing
worldINDIA: A MIDDLE CLASS TOO SMALL TO SUPPLANT CHINA’S ECONOMIC ROLE
INDIA IS STRUGGLING TO ESTABLISH A ROBUST MIDDLE
CLASS
With an annual growth rate of 7.3% over the past decade (excluding the
pandemic), India has one of the highest growth rates among the world’s
major economies. Yet, its middle-class consumer base remains surpri-
singly small. When measured in current U.S. dollars, total consumption

is very high in India (USD 2,241 billion in 2025), but this figure prima -
rily reflects the country’s overall population size. By comparison, this
consumption level falls 3.5 times short of China’s corresponding figure.
Furthermore, per capita consumption adjusted for purchasing power
parity (PPP) is 1.3 times lower than that of Indonesia, the Philippines
and Vietnam. When evaluated at constant prices, consumption per ca-
pita has increased only slightly over the past thirty years compared to

China and even Vietnam (Chart 1).
According to our estimates, based on the research conducted by Ló -
pez-Calva and Ortiz-Juarez (2014) 1, India’s middle-class consumer
segment accounts for just 1.6% of the total population (see Table 1),
or fewer than 24 million people. This is less than in Thailand and Viet-
nam (more than 30 million each) and far below China (approximately
490 million, according to the same definition).
The majority of households identified as middle class by standard In -

dian research institutions (the Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy
[CMIE] and the National Council of Applied Economic Research [NCAER])
would, in fact, be categorised as “vulnerable” according to World Bank
criteria: households that have officially emerged from poverty yet lack
the financial stability necessary for sustained economic security.
Highly concentrated consumption and limited
upward mobility
The structure of Indian household consumption reveals significant

disparities between urban and rural areas. In 2024, average month -
ly consumption per capita amounted to 6,996 rupees in urban areas
1 These authors define the middle class as individuals for whom the risk of falling back into poverty is limited (less than 4% over four years), corresponding to daily per capita income
ranging from 17 to 98 2021 PPP dollars (with vulnerability thresholds at 6.85 and 14 2017 PPP dollars, according to the World Bank). In the absence of reliable national income

data collection mechanisms in India, our study applies these thresholds to the World Bank’s consumer expenditure data, an approach aligned with the objective of assessing actual
purchasing capacity deployed in the market (although this tends to slightly underestimate the middle class in terms of income, as household savings remain unaccounted for in
expenditure measurements).
(approximately USD 84.5), compared with 4,122 rupees in rural areas
(less than USD 50). The wealthiest 5% of urban consumers spent an

average equivalent of USD 245 per month per person. Consequently,
discretionary spending power remained concentrated among the hi -
ghest income brackets.
The structure of consumption has changed over the past decade. The
proportion allocated to food purchases (34.8% of total spending on ave-
rage) declined by 4.3 percentage points between 2011–12 and 2023–
24, while allocations to transportation and communications increased
to 12.4% (+3.8 pp). Although the allocation to durable goods has in -

creased, it remains below 7% of total household spending. This increase
was driven more by a rise in the use of credit than by income growth.
Over the past ten years, consumer loans have been the main source of
household debt. Although still modest relative to GDP , household debt
rose by 12.2 pp to reach 47.8% of GDP by the end of 2025, according to
the Bank for International Settlements (BIS).

THE LABOUR MARKET SUFFERS FROM STRUCTURAL
WEAKNESSES
The limited size of the middle class and the low level of household
consumption in India can be attributed, in particular, to the enduring
structural weaknesses of the labour market. The labour market faces
two major problems: insufficient job creation and the fact that most of
the jobs created are low-skilled and low-paid.

Insufficient job creation
Despite the Indian economy’s robust growth, its employment rate re -
mains substantially lower than in comparable Asian economies: 52.7%
in 2025 according to the International Labour Organization (ILO), com-
pared with 65.6% in Indonesia and 71.3% in Vietnam. This disparity
is mainly due to low labour force participation among women (34%
compared with 53.4% and 68%) and young people (40.8% of those aged
20-24 compared with 66.7% and 69.4%).

Over the past ten years, employment growth has averaged just 2.4%
annually, whereas GDP growth was close to 6% per year.
The services sector (representing 33.2% of employment in 2025) ac -
counted for the bulk of job creation during this period (+3.2% per year),
far ahead of the manufacturing sector (+1.4%) and, above all, agricultu-
re (+0.1%), which nevertheless remains the country’s largest employer
(41.5% of the workforce). This is not enough to absorb the 10 to 12 mil-

lion new entrants to the labour market each year.
2 Employment elasticity remained relatively stable between 1990 and 2012.
A limited number of sectors have significant po-
tential for creating high-quality, well-paid jobs
To identify sectors with high potential for job creation, we calculate
employment elasticity both for aggregate economy (KLEMS data) and
for the formal manufacturing sector alone (Annual Survey of Industries,
2025).
Over the period 1981–2023, aggregate employment elasticity remained

low at a virtually unchanged level, close to 0.3. This stability, however,
masks significant sectoral divergences: elasticity remained stable in
the services sector throughout the period (0.5), but it declined shar -
ply in the manufacturing sector (from 0.4 during 1981–1990 to just
0.1 during 2012–2023 2): at the same growth rate, India now creates
far fewer industrial jobs per unit of growth than it did forty years ago
(Table 2). In the formal manufacturing sector alone, employment elas-

ticity reached 0.7 over the past ten years, driven by very robust job
creation. However, this positive trend has not been sufficient to offset
the decline in informal sector employment, which accounts for 73.4%
of total manufacturing employment but only 38% of the sector’s value
added. Labour productivity in the formal manufacturing sector is thus
estimated to be 5.4 times higher than in the informal sector.
In capital-intensive sectors, employment elasticity ranged from 0.5 to

0.6 (including electrical equipment, transportation, chemicals and me-
tals), whereas in labour-intensive sectors — specifically wood, textiles
and apparel, and manufacturing food — employment elasticities re -
mained particularly low, or in certain cases, negative.
SOURCE: KLEMS (FORMAL AND INFORMAL SECTORS), ILO, BNP PARIBAS CALCULATIONS (LOG-LOG REGRESSION WITH DUMMY VARIABLES FOR THE TWO

Over the 2012–2023 period, India’s strong GDP growth did not translate into job creation in labour-intensive manufacturing sectors

for a changing
world
In other words, growth in value added in these sectors with high labour 
absorption potential is primarily driven by productivity gains rather 
than job creation. Furthermore, even among companies operating in 
the formal sector, employment elasticity remains low at an average of 
0.4. Consequently, the traditional mechanism facilitating labour mobi-
lity from the agricultural sector to the labour-intensive manufacturing 
sector has been very limited over the past forty years.
The only manufacturing subsectors that combine strong growth, high 
employment elasticity, and high productivity — thereby offering real 
potential for creating high-quality jobs — are electronics and infor -
mation technology, electrical equipment, the automotive industry, the 
chemical industry, and, to a lesser extent, the pharmaceutical industry. 
In these subsectors, the formal employment elasticity ranges from 0.8 
to 1.2 (Chart 2). However, in 2025, these jobs accounted for just 1.4% 
of total employment and 5.9% of GDP . The potential therefore remains 
negligible at the macroeconomic level.
Several structural constraints limit job creation in 
India
- The high rigidity of the labour market: it consists mainly of small, in-
formal businesses — those with fewer than 50 employees account for 
65% of the productive sector — largely due to the stringency of labour 
legislation (for example, companies with more than 300 employees 
cannot carry out mass layoffs without authorisation).
- Numerous obstacles impede the growth of industrial and job-crea -
ting businesses: an unfavourable business environment (particularly 
difficulties in accessing land acquisition to build factories); high costs 
of accessing capital (particularly for small and medium-sized enter -
prises); insufficient export competitiveness in labour-intensive sectors 
(textiles, clothing), which encounter disadvantages, including less ad -
vantageous tariff conditions3 than competing Asian economies.
- The mismatch between the skills of the available workforce and the 
needs of businesses. This mismatch constrains wage growth and the 
development of household purchasing power, obstructs industrial ad -
vancement, and, consequently, the creation of high-quality jobs.
INADEQUATE JOB QUALITY DUE TO A LACK OF  
APPROPRIATE SKILLS
A predominantly informal and low-paid labour  
market
According to the ILO, 87.2% of total employment in India is informal, 
with 73.4% concentrated in the manufacturing sector alone, predomi -
nantly engaged in low-value-added activities that require few skills. 
Furthermore, the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) 2023/24 indi -
cates that 58.4% of Indian workers are self-employed, 19.8% are day 
labourers, and only 21.7% are regular employees — the highest-paying 
status, with a wage gap of more than 60% compared to the other two 
categories. According to the ILO, 20% of the population captures 79% of 
aggregate labour income. The agricultural sector, which still employs 
41.5% of the labour force while contributing less than 15% to GDP , is

3 Historically, tariff rates imposed on Indian products exported to Europe have been higher than those applied to certain other Asian countries (Bangladesh, Vietnam, Pakistan).
4 According to the World Bank and the NCAER, educational and training programmes are too theoretical and fail to develop cognitive and interpersonal skills. Vocational education 
and training are not designed to meet the needs of high-demand sectors. Collaborative relationships between educational institutions and employers — particularly regarding 
internships and on-the-job training — remain inadequately developed.
3.7 times less productive than the services sector, with a median hourly 
wage that is 40 to 50% lower.
A widespread skills shortage
This concentration of earnings reflects the low skill levels of the jobs 
held: according to the PLFS, 88.2% of workers hold jobs requiring little 
or no skill. The proportion of skilled jobs is just 11.2% and has declined 
by 4.6 pp since 2018. The underlying challenge is not a shortage of 
graduates but rather a misalignment between what the labour market 
needs and the educational preparation provided to graduates 4, which 
results in their gradual downward mobility into positions far below 
their formal qualifications.
Unemployment, the downward mobility of graduates, and a shortage 
of technical skills
This disparity between skills and business needs directly contributes to 
unemployment among graduates, which reached 13.5% in 2025 accor-
ding to the ILO, compared with 1.3% for individuals without a degree. 
Within the 25- to 34-year-old demographic, the unemployment rate 
stands at 15.2% for graduates (19.2% for those with a master’s de -
gree), compared with an average of 6.1% across this age group. Only 
8.3% of graduates secure positions that genuinely correspond to their 
educational attainment; 53% of bachelor’s degree holders and 36% of 
master’s degree holders are overqualified for their positions.
In low-skilled jobs, the lack of technical training represents a signifi -
cant barrier to youth employment: according to the World Bank’s En -
terprise Survey (2022), 17.8% of traditional service companies and 14% 
of “intermediate” manufacturing companies cite this as a major obsta-
cle. In India’s manufacturing sector, 32.9% of positions are reportedly 
held by underqualified workers, according to the ILO, compared with 
28.8% in Indonesia and just 16.5% in Vietnam.
A direct impediment to industrial advancement
A survey conducted by the Ministry of Skill Development & Entrepre -
neurship across seven key sectors — automotive, textiles, agriculture, 
livestock, IT, commerce and green energy — reveals that the pace of

for a changing
world
workforce training development significantly lags behind industry re -
quirements. This forces companies to train a considerable number of
young graduates themselves before they are ready for the workforce.
According to the Telecom Talent in the 5G Era report by the Telecom
Sector Skill Council, only 40% of IT and telecommunications graduates
are actually immediately employable.

This disparity in skills particularly constrains the transition towards
higher-value-added manufacturing activities within the sectors iden -
tified above (automotive, electronics and chemicals). Their growth
trajectory depends on technological advancement — electric vehicles,
semiconductors, specialty chemicals — and therefore on intermediate
to advanced technical skills that are currently lacking. Should trai -
ning provision fail to align with industry demand, employment elasti -

city and growth in these sectors will stagnate or decline, undermining
the only source of high-quality job creation currently available — and
consequently, the Indian economy’s ability to expand its middle class.
These structural limitations affecting national industrial development
diminish its appeal to foreign investors, despite its size. The stock of
foreign investment in India stood at USD 560 billion in Q2 2026, repre-
senting just 15% of GDP (compared with 20% in China, more than 52%

in Vietnam, and around 67% of GDP in Thailand and Malaysia).
ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE: A NEW RISK TO JOB CREATION
Artificial intelligence (AI) may also limit the expansion of high-paid
employment opportunities in the service sector. Its deployment poses
a significant threat to employment in India, particularly in the hi -
ghest-paying service sectors — the same sectors that possessed si -
gnificant potential for expanding India’s middle-class consumer base.

Agriculture (41.5% of the labour force), construction (12%), and trade/
transportation (18%) remain largely immune to the direct replacement
of workers by AI, which reduces the overall macroeconomic scale of
the coming shock. The service sectors offering the most lucrative em -
ployment — IT, finance and insurance — account for just 1.3% and 1.2%
of total employment, yet these remain the primary targets of automa-
tion. In IT services companies, the adoption of AI is expected to reduce

the need for entry-level roles (quality assurance, software testing and
support roles) but increase demand for positions centred on platform
development and management oversight. Current sector-specific evi -
dence indicates a notable slowdown in job creation within IT services
but no widespread contraction. A study by the Indian Council for Re -
search on International Economic Relations (ICRIER) on the IT sector
confirms a decline in job creation for routine and entry-level roles.

While new professions are emerging such as data centre operators, AI
DevOps engineers who serve as intermediaries between software deve-
lopment and systems operations teams, and ethical AI specialists, the
volume of employment opportunities generated remains substantially
lower than the number of jobs at risk. Furthermore, the skill require -
ments for these newly created positions exceed those possessed by the
existing cohort of graduates.
REFORMS ARE A STEP IN THE RIGHT DIRECTION BUT DO

NOT GO FAR ENOUGH
Since 2019, the Modi government has implemented a series of mea -
sures to develop industry, attract foreign direct investment, and gain
greater integration in global trade (after decades of protectionist po -
licies).
Corporate subsidy programmes (Production-Linked Incentives) have
helped support the development of those sectors with the greatest
potential for job creation (electrical, electronics, chemicals, and auto -
motive). Similarly, labour market reform (which came into force in late

2025) may alleviate impediments to business expansion and the crea-
tion of formal jobs. However, its actual impact will depend on its adop-
tion by the states, which retain considerable discretionary autonomy
over labour law, and on its effective implementation on the ground.
The free trade agreement signed between India and the European
Union in late January 2026 (which is expected to take effect in 2027 at
the earliest) should mitigate the tariff disadvantage experienced by la-

bour-intensive industries: the EU has pledged to remove tariffs, which
currently range from 4% to 12% on textiles and clothing.
However, these measures alone will be inadequate for creating
high-quality jobs if the education system fails to meet the needs of
Indian businesses. It is therefore imperative that India continues to
improve its business environment and, even more importantly, reform
its education system to promote job creation in high-value-added and

well-paying sectors. Without concrete measures, the “demographic
window” — which is expected to remain favourable for another 15 to
25 years — could close before India establishes a robust industrial
foundation and a substantial middle class.

*****
While India’s economic growth has demonstrated considerable dy -
namism, this growth has not translated into the development of the
middle class. The real medium-term concern involves the emergence
of a “neo-precarious” middle class — substantial in terms of num -
bers but lacking the foundations necessary for advancing towards a
high-income economy (households with stable employment, the ability

to save, and social protection). The creation of formal, high-quality jobs
is currently inadequate to sustain industrial expansion and its progres-
sion towards higher-value segments. The cultivation of certain niche
activities, while promising, remains very limited and is far from benefi-
ting the entire population. Finally, the development of AI poses more of
a risk than a genuine opportunity for the country’s development. India
lacks both the infrastructure and the educational system necessary to

deploy AI in a way that would yield tangible benefits for its economy
and labour market.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

Document 406: Rates Spark_ Back to a 5% 10Y UST on inflation fears

### Page 1

US Treasury bonds were quick to sell oﬀ as US CPI surprised to
the upside. We think EUR and GBP rates should be wary
of blindly following US inﬂation prints, as the economic
backdrop diﬀers signiﬁcantly. We challenge the market's
assumption that the ECB and Bank of England should
automatically follow a more hawkish Fed
Michiel Tukker
Impact from hot US inﬂation was ampliﬁed
by Fed comments
An unpleasant US inﬂation surprise for rates, taking the 10Y UST

yield right back to 5%, is a justiﬁed move in our view. A core CPI
reading of 0.3% MoM annualises to a yearly inﬂation number well
above 3% and far from the Fed’s target. Add additional uncertainty
from energy prices and AI investments, and markets are right to be
nervous. Last week’s solid payrolls report also gives little reason for
the Fed to choose a more dovish path for now.

### Page 2

Following on from Fed Governor Chris Waller's comment that he
backs a 25bp hike in December and could opt for a 50bp hike,
Treasury yields pushed even higher, with the 10yr up 6bp and 2yr
up 13bp since before Waller's comments. Markets are now more
than fully pricing in a 25bp hike in December, in line with our own
forecast, versus 20bp from before Waller's comments.
Nonetheless, the hopes of a Middle East de-escalation are pulling
energy costs lower, with retail gasoline prices now below $4/gallon

for the ﬁrst time since mid-July. This will certainly help give a
subdued November inﬂation print, but we won't get that until the
day after the December FOMC meeting. The Fed may get a sneak
peek, and that should give it the conﬁdence to only raise rates by
25bp. We are not forecasting any further hikes in 2027 as interest
rate-sensitive sectors cool further, with housing costs and lower
energy prices, amidst weak wage growth, dampening price
pressures in the economy.

European rates shouldn't follow hawkish US
repricing blindly
Euro and sterling rates quickly followed the Fed's hawkish repricing,
but we think those moves could fade fast. Interestingly, the 2Y EUR
inﬂation swap fell on the day, suggesting the spillovers were purely
in real terms. In other words, markets are quick to translate tighter
Fed policy into tighter ECB policy. But the reality is more complex.
Both regions face very diﬀerent inﬂation paths, whereby Europe is

more exposed to energy prices and the US to AI-related growth. A
tight correlation between US and European rates is warranted if
driven by oil as the common denominator, but less so if triggered
by macro data.
In addition, higher US rates imply tighter ﬁnancial conditions for
Europe, which means the ECB or Bank of England may not need to
act as aggressively as currently priced. Many ECB speakers have
indeed made the link between higher longer-dated interest rates

and the reduced need to increase policy rates. In other words,
markets are already doing the tightening. We therefore prefer to

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM)  (5 
November)

As written in our Macro Strategy note , 
BNM’s final meeting of 2026 on 5 
November will be live and a close call, 
after the central bank opened the door to 
monetary policy normalisation during its 
September review. BNM Governor Abdul 
Rasheed Ghaffour reiterated in a 
Bloomberg interview on 15 Oc tober that 
the central bank has space to adjust 
policy if needed, as policymakers are 
vigilant about upside inflationary 
pressures amid strong economic growth. 
He also said that policy decisions will be 
data dependent. We think the latest 
available Septemb er data for the 
upcoming meeting suggest that 
policymakers can afford to be patient 
until 1H27, maintaining the Overnight 
Policy Rate at 2.75% for now. Inflation 
remained contained within the lower half 
of the official 2026 inflation forecast 
range of 1.5 -2.5%, notwithstanding 
external price pressures and robust 
economic activity. Advance estimates 
showed GDP growth of 5.3% yoy in 3Q26, 
while exports jumped to a five -year high 
of 54.5% yoy in September, 
supercharged by the artificial 
intelligence boom. However, headline 
inflation eased slightly to 1.8% yoy in 
September 2026 from 1.9% yoy in August, 
while core inflation remained stable at 1.7% 
yoy. Fuel subsidies have limited the pass-
through of upstream cost pressures to 
consumer prices, alongside stable 
domestic demand conditions.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

South Korea

October trade and inflation data are due 
for release. Export growth is expected to 
accelerate to nearly 100% yoy, up from 
83.7% in September, while the trade 
surplus is forecast to remain above 
USD40bn for another month. CPI 
inflation is expected to hold steady at 2.9% 
yoy. Overall, the data should reinforce 
the view that AI - and semiconductor -
driven growth and the expansion in 
South Korea’s trade surplus remain 
firmly on track, providing fundamental 
support for the KOSPI and KRW. 
Meanwhile, inflation remains sticky 
above the BOK’s 2% target, bolstering 
the case for further monetary tightening 
as early as the November policy meeting.

Taiwan

October inflation data are forthcoming. 
Headline CPI inflation is expected to 
remain above 2% for the sixth 
consecutive month, at 2.3% yoy. Core 
CPI inflation is also likely to stay above 2% 
for the sixth consecutive month, at 2.3% 
yoy. Despite government subsidies on 
fuel and electricity prices, higher global 
energy prices continue to feed through 
to CPI via items such as airfares and tour 
package fees. Services inflation also 
remains sticky, particularly in food away 
from home, housin g, transportation, and 
entertainment. Meanwhile, domestic 
inflation expectations appear to be 
gradually rising. A recent private -sector 
survey showed that the public expects

inflation to average 2.5% in 2026, with 
52% of respondents expecting inflation 
to exceed 2.5%. Against this backdrop, 
we maintain our view that the central 
bank will resume rate hikes in December, 
raising the policy rate by 12.5  bps to 
2.125%.

Thailand

We expect Thailand’s headline inflation 
to rise to 3.2% yoy  in October 2026, 
extending the increase over the previous 
two months, and moving slightly above 
the upper end of the Bank of Thailand’s 
1-3% target range. This was likely driven 
by faster energy inflation amid an 
uptrend in diesel prices, alongside a 
rebound in food inflation. The inflation 
uptrend was likely broadly in line with 
the central bank's expectations and 
mainly supply-side driven, supporting its 
decision to look through the temporary 
price pressures and keep its policy rate 
unchanged at its October meeting.

Vietnam

We expect Vietnam’s October 2026 
goods exports and retail sales to remain 
robust at 42.0% yoy and 14.0% yoy year-
to-date, respectively, while headline 
inflation is likely to stay elevated at 
around 5.0% yoy. Exports have 
continued to be supported by strong  
electronics and machinery shipments 
amid a favourable technology cycle with 
spillovers from robust artificial 
intelligence-related demand. Retail 
sales were likely bolstered by firm 
household spending, supported by 
favourable labour market conditions, 
and continued strength in tourism. 
Headline inflation has remained elevated 
and above the central bank’s target of 
4.5% for the year, driven by price 
pressures from transport and energy, 
food, as well as material costs. This 
poses a challenge for policymakers as 
they seek to balance inflati on risks 
against ambitions of achieving at least 10% 
economic growth this year.

Hong Kong

Hong Kong’s economy is expected to 
decelerate from 4.3% yoy in Q2 to 2.6% 
in Q3, largely due to slower private 
consumption. Despite a 13% increase in 
Mainland visitor arrivals during the 
period, domestic consumption continued 
to be weighed down by a weaken ing 
labour market, with the unemployment 
rate elevated at 3.9% in September. 
Meanwhile, higher interest rates have 
dampened investment sentiment and 
capital market activity. The Centa -City 
Leading Index remained broadly flat, 
while the Hang Seng Index was range-
bound at around 25,000. The positive 
wealth effect seen in 1H has faded as 
asset market performance remained 
muted. A strong HKD continued to 
encourage outbound tourism, evidenced 
by an increase in average daily resident 
departures from 6.9% yoy in Q2 to 10.9% 
in Q3.

However, GDP growth is expected to be 
buffered by improving net exports amid 
the ongoing AI -electronics upcycle. 
Export growth picked up and outpaced 
import growth during Q3. Hong Kong, as 
China’s free port and re -export hub, is 
benefiting from stronger de mand for 
Chinese electronic goods.

Indonesia/ Philippines

### Page 4

The Week Ahead October 30, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         4

Indonesia’s 3Q GDP likely moderated to 
5.2% y/y. Soft consumer confidence and 
retail sales growth point to weaker 
consumption, compounded by a gradual 
rise in inflation. Fiscal measures likely 
helped spending among low -income 
households. Despite the quarte rly 
moderation, we maintain our full -year 
growth forecast at 5.3% y/y.

Inflation rates in Indonesia and the 
Philippines are expected to rise, albeit to 
different degrees. Indonesia’s inflation is 
on course to edge up modestly, while in 
the Philippines, stronger pass -through 
from higher oil prices, food and 
transportation costs, as well as imported 
inflation pressures stemming from a 
weaker peso, is likely to push inflation to 
a multi-year high.

產業聚焦降息前的美國汽車產業投資布局
2026/10/30
• 美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款
週期
• 高油價使油電、電動車需求
提升
• 自駕車、軟體營收增加帶動
車商獲利
美國傳統車商迎來皮卡改款週期，獲利表現有望提升
美國傳統車廠近年轉向生產更多高價卡車和SUV，因部分SUV和皮卡的調整後息稅前利潤率高達20%、超
過其他車型，也使美國新車平均交易價格在9月超過5萬美元。福特與通用汽車即將在2026年年底至2027
年迎來全尺寸皮卡改款與更新計畫，由於在推出新款或新一代卡車後，通常會有2~3年較佳的銷售期，預
估福特和通用汽車在2027年及之後的獲利有機會持續提升。
美國政策轉為支持燃油車發展，但高油價使油電、電動車需求提升

儘管美國政策轉向支持燃油車發展 ，但自美伊戰爭後油價居高不下 ，使油電混合車3Q26在美國的市佔率
暴衝至16%，包括豐田 、現代等品牌在美國的市占率持續上升 。美國3Q26電動車銷量約佔新車總銷量
6.5%，高於2Q26的5.8%，顯示電動車在美國市場正重新站穩腳步 。由於油價上升使全球對電動車需求
增加，特斯拉2Q26、3Q26交車量也優於市場預期。
自駕車接受度提升，軟體營收增加帶動車商獲利
美國消費者對自動駕駛的認識與接受度正持續提升，而各車商正積極擴大自駕與軟體訂閱服務布局。通用
汽車預估其OnStar和Super Cruise自駕輔助營收在2026年將突破30億美元，福特汽車的軟體付費訂閱用
戶已達160萬戶。3Q26特斯拉FSD活躍用戶數已達174萬、年增67%，且北美新車FSD採用率超過75%。
相較傳統車商低於10%的硬體銷售利潤率，軟體毛利率高達50~70%，且訂閱制使車商部分營收轉為持續
性收入，將帶動其估值表現。

美國9月份輕型車年化銷售量約為
1,600萬輛，低於市場預估共識的
1,630萬輛 。儘管9月初的勞動節
促銷活動使購車回饋及零售銷售
增加，但同時銷售量年減 4%也顯
示市場需求正面臨更大挑戰。
今年上半年儘管面臨高利率 、高
車價等負面影響 ，但高收入族群
支撐使美國車市仍具韌性 。然而
隨著利率上升 、對消費者可負擔
性的懷疑日益增加 ，市場目前正
逐步將2026年美國輕型車銷售量
預估由 1,610萬輛下修至 1,560至
1,600萬輛區間。
聯準會在 9月升息一碼 ，使美國
目標利率區間上升至 3.75~4%，
且目前 市場普遍預期聯準會在今
年12月及2027年上半年將再各升
息一碼 。美國約有 75~85%新車
購買使用貸款或融資 ，利率持續
上升將直接推高車貸利率 ，進而
增加消費者的購車成本與負債壓
力，且利率上升也會造成核貸門
檻轉趨嚴格 ，使消費者對新車的

需求下降。
未來若油價 、通膨下降 ，市場重
新步入降息循環後有望使美國車
市需求重新回升。資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/9/24，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/22

車商將迎來皮卡改款週期，有望提升獲利表現

資料來源：Cox Automotive，2026/10/13，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10/22

Document 515: バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持

Source PDF: all report pdf/バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 6 日

MURC Focus

バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持

～10 月末発表の秋季予算案で具体的な財源を示せるか～

調査部 主任研究員 土田 陽介

1. 伝統的な財政拡張を訴えるバーナム新政権 
英国では、9 月 27 日から 30 日にかけて、北西部の主要都市リバプールで与党である労働党の党大会が開催
された。7 月 20 日に就任したアンディ・バーナム首相にとって初の党大会となると同時に、新政権の財政運営方
針が示されるという点で、この党大会は非常に高い注目を浴びた。この場で示されたのは、中道左派である労働
党らしい、伝統的な財政拡張路線に基づくプランだった。

具体的には、バーナム 新政権の関係閣僚らは、政府調達（政府が民間からモノやサービスを購入すること）の
増強による景気浮揚策を主張した。例えばジョン・ヒーリー財務相は 28 日の党大会で、スコットランドのクライド海
軍基地に浮きドック 3 基を建設する他、海洋調査船を建造することに数十億ポンドを投資する計画を発表した。
いずれも国内の業者に発注することで雇用や所得につなげる方針である。

また労働党大会に先立つ 26 日、バーナム新政権は、新築住宅を初めて購入する国民向けに新たな住宅購
入支援策を導入すると発表した。保守党政権下の 2013 年から 2023 年にかけて実施された“Help to Buy”と似
たスキームであり、購入者は購入価格の 2.5%に相当する頭金を支払えば、政府が購入価格の 20%までを融資
するというものである。

このように、バーナム新政権が目指す方針は、労働党に伝統的な“大きな政府”による財政拡張路線を反映し
ている。そうした点で、バーナム新政権の方針はキア・スターマー前政権とはあまり変化がない。一方で、新政権
が示した財政拡張路線が金融市場の評価を得るためには、財源を明示する必要がある。10 月 28 日に発表する
秋季予算案で、金融市場が納得する内容を示せるかが鍵を握る。

〇9月末にリバプールで開催された労働党の党大会で、バーナム新政権の財政運営方針が示されたが 、それ
は中道左派である労働党らしい伝統的な財政拡張路線に基づくプランだった。 
〇一方、金融市場 では、それまではバーナム 人気によって労働党 の政権運営が安定するとの思惑から“英国
買い”が先行していたが、党大会前後から調整色を濃くしている。 
〇増税は有権者の反発を、国債発行が投資家の反発を生むと予想される中、秋季予算で財政拡張の財源の
筋道をきちんと示せなければ、バーナム新政権も短命で終わりかねない。

### Page 2

2. 財政拡張を続けてきた近年の英国 
バーナム新政権は財政拡張に活路を 見いだそうとしているが、これまでも英国の政権は財政拡張に臨んでき
た（図表 1）。例えば英国の実質 GDP（国内総生産）に占める公需（＝政府支出＋公的固定資本形成）は、保守
党政権（テリーザ・メイ元首相）期の 2018 年後半に 21.5%を下回るまで低下したが、以降は上昇し、直近 2026
年 4～6 月時点で 24.4%となっている（図表 1）。

この間、2020 年 1～3 月期に生じたコロナショック後の公需の減退も限定的にとどまっており、結局は公需が
GDP に占める割合は“V 字回復”することとなった。労働党が政権を奪取したのは 2024 年 7 月の総選挙だが、
それ以前の保守党政権下でも、英国は事実上、財政拡張を続けていたことになる。その結果、公的債務残高の
対 GDP 比率も、一時は高インフレのために低下したが、再び上昇した（図表 2）。

このように近年の英国は、保革の立場を問わず財政拡張を続けてきた。しかし、EU 離脱やエネルギー不安に
伴う供給制約の高まりの中でそうした手法を取り続けたため 、英国はスタグフレーション（景気停滞と物価高進の
併存）に突入した。その改善のために必要な処方箋は、規制緩和を通じた供給の刺激である。しかし、それは“小
さな政府”を追求することにもなるため、中道左派として“大きな政府”を目指す労働党は容認できない。

バーナム新首相は地方の主導の経済成長を訴え、そうした意味での分権化に意欲的と考えられる。 ただし労
働党としては、公需による民需の刺激を通じた景気浮揚を引き続き志向していることが、秋の党大会で示された
形である。一方、金融市場では、バーナム新首相就任直後は、首相の人気に支えられて新政権の運営が安定す
るとの思惑から“英国買い”が先行していたが、党大会前後から調整色を濃くしている。

### Page 3

3. 財源なき財政拡張で信用を失うリスク 
スターマー前政権もまた、財政拡張路線による景気浮揚を目指した が、同時にそれは、供給の底上げを狙うも
のでもあった。老朽インフラ（鉄道や水道など）の再開発はその最たるものである。またスターマー前政権は、財政
規律にも配慮し、財源を国債発行よりも増税でカバーし、 財政拡張策の整合性を取ろう とした。財源を示したこと
を投資家は評価したが、増税に有権者が反発したため、結局、スターマー前政権は短命に終わった。

スターマー前政権下で低迷した労働党の支持率は、バーナム人気に支えられ“V 字回復”し、右派の改革党
（UK Reform ）と肉薄するに至る（図表 3）。労働党としては打ち出した目玉政策を着実に遂行し、有権者の支持
をさらに確たるものにしたいところである。とはいえ、そうした目玉政策は財政拡張を伴うものであるため、財源をど
う確保するか明確な筋道を示さなければ、金融市場の動揺を誘う。

すでに英国の長期金利（10 年国債流通利回り）は、グローバルなインフレ加速懸念に伴う世界的な金利上昇
の波に飲まれ、上昇が続いている（図表 4）。金融市場は財政拡張に対して神経を尖らせているが、秋季予算で
明確な財源が示さなければ、それを嫌気した投資家が英国債（ギルト債）の売却を進め、金利が一段と上昇し、
場合によってポンド安と株安の“トリプル安”を引き起こす可能性もある。

財政拡張の財源を増税で賄おうとすると、スターマー前政権のように有権者の支持を失いかねない。 一方、国
債発行で賄おうとすると、長期金利の押し上げ圧力になる。ゆえに、歳出の見直しで財源を賄おうとするだろうが、
簡単ではないため、具体的な筋道を示せなければ、結局は金利の上昇につながる。秋季予算で財政拡張の財
源の筋道をきちんと示せなければ、バーナム新政権も短命で終わりかねない。

維持評等  
買進(Buy) 
Interposer 貢獻優於預期，VSMC P1 損平點可望提前  
⚫ 3Q26財報、 4Q26財測皆優於預期  
⚫ 產能利用率續增 /漲價 /較佳產品組合有助 減輕 VSMC折舊衝擊  
⚫ AI營收佔比與產品市佔 有望持續提升，維持買進建議  
3Q26財報優於預期：受惠 ASP提升及晶圓出貨優於財測 ，世界 3Q26
營收 157億元 (QoQ +10%、YoY+ 27%)；毛利率則因電源管理占比增至
82%驅動產品組合轉佳 、產能利用率升至 90%、與有利匯率挹注達
33.6 %；營益率 22.1%、EPS  1.6元，財報表現 優於市場預期。 
4Q26營收預估呈高個位數季增 ：受限產能瓶頸，公司預期 4Q26晶圓
出貨量僅季增 0~2 %；ASP則季增 3~5%，高於上季指引，我們認為主
要仍反映成本提升 ；毛利率 34~36%，整體財測優於預期，產能利用
率也預期在終端需求支撐下續增至 90%+。我們另考慮 4Q26匯率區間
可望高於公司預期，估營收達 171億元、毛利率 35.5%、EPS  1.85元。 
AI原動力未變，預期 2027年漲價趨勢延續 ：關於近期 AI展望調整，
管理層認為全球需求並未有任何減少且趨勢並不可逆，只是可能改以
更合理的成長型態發生 。漲價議題上， 除提到不能讓客戶感受不佳，
更希望能讓整個供應鏈都能承擔， 但 2027 年漲價幅度仍有望優於今
年，我們認為主因在於原物料成本、及公司產能利率持續上揚所致。  
VSMC 雖折舊壓力沉重但 Interposer 將有助提前損平 ：VSMC  P1方
面，除重申將自 1Q27量產外，也預期相關折舊可能造成 1H27獲利一
定衝擊，2H27起則隨規模提升逐步減輕。我們認為，雖預期 VSMC P1
將對 1H27毛利率產生高個位數稀釋效果，但預期在 全年 (1)ASP 漲幅
估達雙位數、 (2)產能利用率估近 95%、(3)高毛利率之 Interposer 有望
佔 12吋營收逾 50%，故維持對公司 2027年毛利率近 32%預期。我們
更預期在 VSMC P1 已被 LTA完整覆蓋、及 Interposer 推動此廠損平
點提前來臨下， P2最快也將於 2027年中前宣布展開計畫。 
展望依舊積極且評價已然低檔 ，維持「買進」評等：我們基於
Interposer 對 VSMC P1 帶來之效益優於預期、與更為樂觀的匯率
假設小幅上修公司 2026 /2027 年 EPS 至 6.3 /7.6 元。雖因盤勢 轉
弱改給予較低評價倍數，但 基於中長期展望依舊積極， 故維持「買
進」建議，目標價 調整至 18 0元 (2027 年 EPS* 24倍 PE R)。  
風險因子：漲價程度低於預期 。

Document 534: 世界对黄仁勋依赖过深，英伟达应降低单一CEO风险

Source PDF: all report pdf/世界对黄仁勋依赖过深，英伟达应降低单一CEO风险.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

FT 主编 Khalaf 撰⽂称，  ⻩仁勋私⽣活丑闻暴露的不只是个⼈问题 —— 这家市值 4.5 万亿美
元、占标普 500 指数 7% 权重的公司，已具备 “ 系统重要性 ” ，堪⽐华尔街⼤型银⾏。更危险
的是，⻩仁勋直管约 60 名下属、⽆明确接班⼈。建议效仿亚⻢逊、苹果模式，设⽴实质性
“ ⼆号位 ” ，并预⾔相关监管议题或将于 2027 年进⼊国会议程。
摘要：
11 ⽉ 9 ⽇，英国《⾦融时报》（ FT ）总编辑 Roula Khalaf 在 FT 发表了⼀篇个⼈观点⽂章，直指当
前全球科技产业对英伟达 CEO ⻩仁勋的过度依赖问题。⽂章以⻩仁勋近期陷⼊的个⼈丑闻为切
⼊点，深刻揭⽰了⼀家市值 4.5 万亿美元的科技巨头在公司治理上存在的结构性隐患。
Khalaf 在⽂章开篇便点明了问题的严峻性 ——⼀位⾏为失当的 CEO 对任何公司来说都是⼤⿇
烦，但英伟达⾼层爆发的这场丑闻，波及的远不⽌这⼀家公司本⾝。⻩仁勋本⼈于近⽇公开承
认对妻⼦不忠。鉴于英伟达知识产权的巨⼤价值，这⼀事件引发了⼤量需要由董事会委托外部

律师介⼊调查的严肃问题。
从更宏观的视⻆来看，英伟达的问题绝⾮⼀家公司的内部事务。这家公司占标普 500 指数近 7%
的权重，其⼀举⼀动都牵动着整个市场的神经。与此同时，⻩仁勋还通过持股、担保贷款、为
数据中⼼建设⽅提供融资等⽅式，深度介⼊了 OpenAI 、 Anthropic 等众多 AI 实验室的成⻓链条，
成为整个 AI ⽣态的重要⽀撑者。
Khalaf 的核⼼论点是：世界对⻩仁勋个⼈的依赖已经过深，英伟达必须认真考虑降低 “ 单⼀ CEO
⻛险 ”。她在⽂章中明确指出， " 单⼀故障点对⼀家⼤公司来说从来都不是好事 " ，并提出了⼀系
列切实可⾏的改⾰建议。
丑闻背后：英伟达的治理隐患有多深？
Khalaf 在⽂章中指出，⻩仁勋⽬前的管理负荷已经相当沉重 ——他直接管辖的汇报对象多达约
60 ⼈，且没有明显的接班⼈选。这种⾼度集中的权⼒结构，本⾝就是⼀种潜在⻛险。
更令⼈担忧的是英伟达董事会的现状。根据咨询公司 Spencer Stuart 的数据，英伟达董事会成员
的平均任期⻓达 18 年，超过标普 500 均值的两倍。其中有五位董事的任期约达三⼗年，⽽这⼀现

象显然未曾引发股东的有效抵制。
Khalaf 认为，这样的董事会结构谈不上 “ 强健 ” ，在关键时刻能否发挥有效的监督和纠偏作⽤，令
⼈存疑。
英伟达的 “ 系统性 ” 地位：为何牵⼀发⽽动全⾝？
那么，英伟达的问题为何会成为 " 全球性 " 问题？ Khalaf 给出了清晰的逻辑链。
⾸先是体量。英伟达市值⾼达 4.5 万亿美元，在标普 500 指数中占⽐接近 7% ，任何⻛吹草动都会
直接影响市场整体表现。其次是⽣态渗透。⻩仁勋不仅是芯⽚卖家，更是整个 AI 产业链的 " 背书
⼈ "—— 他通过持股、担保贷款、为数据中⼼建设⽅融资等⽅式，将英伟达的命运与 OpenAI 、
Anthropic 等头部 AI 机构深度绑定。第三是情绪⽀撑 Khalaf 特别提到，在科技投资者信⼼动摇之
际，⻩仁勋曾多次充当 " 啦啦队⻓ " ，最近⼀次是在所谓 "SaaS 末⽇ " 软件股抛售潮期间。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
正因如此， Khalaf 认为英伟达已经具备了某种 “ 系统性 ” 特征，与华尔街⼤型银⾏的地位颇为相
似。 “ 英伟达远没有华尔街银⾏那么复杂，但它正在变得同样具有系统重要性。 ” 她在⽂章中如此
写道。
如何破局？现成的模板并不少
⾯对这⼀困局， Khalaf 并⾮只是提出问题，她也给出了具体的解决思路。
她⾸先援引了多个成功的先例。摩根⼠丹利、亚⻢逊、苹果等公司都曾将功勋卓著的 CEO 转任
董事⻓，同时为继任者的成⻓留出空间。⾕歌 DeepMind 创始⼈德⽶斯 · 哈萨⽐斯（ Demis
Hassabis ）近期也从 CEO 职位退⾄董事⻓⻆⾊， “ 给⾃⼰更多空间思考 AI 的宏观图景 ” 。这些案
例表明，权⼒的平稳过渡并⾮不可实现。
即便英伟达暂时不考虑让⻩仁勋卸任 CEO ， Khalaf 也建议公司设⽴实质性的 “ ⼆号位”—— ⽐如

总裁或⾸席运营官 —— 让投资者清楚地知道，⼀旦 “ ⽼板出了意外 ” ，有⼈能够接棒。她直接点
名： “ 英伟达也应该这样做。 ”
监管层⾯：⼤科技公司是否该像银⾏⼀样被约束？
Khalaf 在⽂章中还抛出了⼀个更具前瞻性的问题：⻓期以来被视为 “ 基础社会设施 ” 的银⾏业所承
受的监管压⼒，是否也应该适⽤于⼤型科技公司？
她指出，像摩根⼤通这样的银⾏，在监管机构的严密盯防下，别⽆选择，只能建⽴完善的应急
预案。相⽐之下，英伟达⽬前尚未⾯临类似的外部约束。但随着其系统重要性⽇益凸显，这⼀
局⾯或许不会持续太久。 Khalaf 预测，这⼀议题很可能会出现在 2027 年新⼀届国会的议程上。
这⼀判断并⾮空⽳来⻛。当⼀家公司的兴衰⾜以撼动整个市场，当其 CEO 的个⼈⾏为可能牵涉
国家安全，外部监管介⼊的逻辑便已成⽴。
市场已经在 “ ⽤脚投票 ”
有意思的是，资本市场对这⼀事件的反应，似乎已经在悄然验证 Khalaf 的判断。
英伟达竞争对⼿ AMD 的股价在事件曝光后的周⼀单⽇上涨了 12% ；新兴芯⽚制造商 Cerebras 也

上涨了 5% 。 这⼀市场反应表明，投资者已经开始主动思考⼀个 " 不那么以英伟达为中⼼ " 的世
界。
Khalaf 也承认，客⼾的反应通常⽐市场慢，但她认为，⻩仁勋此次的 " 失误 " 同样给了客⼾⼀个重
新审视供应链依赖的契机。⽆论最终结果如何，这场⻛波都是⼀次有价值的警⽰。
回到⽂章的核⼼论点， Khalaf 的结论简洁⽽有⼒：“ 单⼀故障点对⼀家⼤公司来说从来都不是好
事。 ”这句话既是对英伟达现状的诊断，也是对整个科技⾏业的警⽰。

### Page 1

十一月 10, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  IC 設計服務 ‧ 台灣

世芯-KY 
(3661.TW /3661 TT) 
2027 年營收倍增可期，2nm 接棒 2028 年成長 
重要訊息 
世芯-KY 3Q26 EPS 創歷史新高，並宣布再取得 10 億美元等級的大型設計案。
公司預期 4Q26營收再創新高，2027年營收將呈三位數成長，2nm專案亦將如
期於 4Q26開始 tape-out，我們認為世芯 2027-28年的成長能見度明顯提高。 
評論及分析  
3nm 專案 allocation 提高。美系 CSP 3nm AI 加速器已順利進入大量生
產，晶片符合所有測試標準且良率合理。公司預期此專案 2027 年營收將年
增三位數，優於先前指引，主要來自客戶提高對世芯的 allocation 。公司亦
表示 3nm 將量產約兩年才交棒下一代，加上雲端晶片走向對外銷售使單一
平台的生命週期拉長，我們預期出貨將延續至 2028 年並與 2nm 量產銜接。  
2nm 專案如期推進，地位較 3nm 更強。次世代 2nm AI 加速器已進入 final 
implementation 階段， 4Q26 起展開一系列 tape-out，目標 4Q27 小量量
產、2028 年初大量量產。2nm 採 chiplet 架構與 GAA 製程，設計附加價值
明顯高於 3nm，世芯負責包含 I/O die 在內所有 die 的 physical design 與整
合，且客戶目前承諾 2nm 晶圓將只由客戶本身與世芯兩家下單，世芯的量
產份額將有望優於 3nm。我們估計 2nm 專案 ASP 將達 3nm 專案的 2 倍以
上。此外，我們預期 A14 專案的 NRE 將自 2027 年開始貢獻，並估計美系
CSP 專案的 turnkey 營收將占 2027 年總營收的 68-70%。 
新取得 10 億美元等級大型設計案， 2Q27 起貢獻量產營收。 此案合約已簽
署，客戶為首次合作的美系企業，晶片用於 HPC。公司預期此案自 2Q27 起
貢獻量產營收並逐季延續，毛利率合理， 2027 年即成為第二大營收來源，
且屬可持續的新商業模式。公司表示產業工程人力全面短缺，許多專案需要
physical design 支援，世芯即以工程能力切入。我們在設計服務產業報告中
指出，客戶自建團隊的速度跟不上工作量成長，front -end 能力強但 back-
end 人力不足的客戶，最適合將 back- end physical design 與量產委由設計
服務業者，此案正反映此一分段委外趨勢。  
投資建議  
市場對世芯最大的疑慮在於營收集中於大客戶，以及世代交替造成的營收波
動，法說會兩項疑慮皆獲正面回應，而 關鍵在資產負債表可承接的規模。公
司指出產業持續走向 COT 模式，屬長期結構性趨勢且有利世芯，與我們在
產業報告中提出客戶由全包轉向分段委外的看法一致。我們調整 2026-27 年
EPS 預估為 124.20 元及 219.12 元，並預估 2028 年 EPS 為 409.40 元。我
們目標價 6,600 元係基於 30 倍(五年均值) 2027 年 EPS。目前股價約當 16 倍
2027 年 EPS 預估，我們認為評價具吸引力，維持「 增加持股」評等。 
投資風險  
美系 CSP 對 AI ASIC 需求轉弱；2nm 專案 tape-out 或量產時程遞延；新客
戶量產進度不如預期；供應鏈瓶頸延續；競爭態勢激烈。 
增加持股‧維持

獲利。

### Page 2

台灣 世芯-KY

十一月 10, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 2

其他專案如期推進，供應瓶頸逐步改善。 網通與 CPU 專案皆如期進行，我
們預期網通 3nm 專案於 2027 年量產，2nm 於 2027 年 tape-out 並自 2028
年起貢獻營收。中國車廠 ADAS 專案需求穩健，次世代設計將如期於 4Q26 
tape- out，我們預期採 5nm 車用製程。不過，載板與封裝產能優先供應 CSP
業務，使車用與消費性等業務出貨受限，公司估計若無此限制，3Q26 營收
可再多約 1 億美元，目前情況正逐步改善。  
毛利率稀釋並非負面訊號，營運槓桿推升獲利。 相較 1Q26，3Q26 毛利率
因 3nm 營收占比偏高、高毛利量產業務受限及部分 NRE 認列遞延而降至
20%以下，但受惠營運槓桿，營益率僅溫和下滑。公司認為 3Q26 毛利率已
接近此營收組合下的最差情境，4Q26 將與 3Q26 相近，長期營益率目標為
high- teens。如我們在產業報告中所述，評估設計服務業者應以營業利益金
額與 EPS 為主。我們預估 2027 年營收將年增 100.0%至新台幣 1,384 億
元，毛利率雖稀釋至 18.8%，但營業利益 、營益率將分別年增 90.8%及年
減 0.7 個百分點。

圖 9：公司概況  圖 10：營收占比(按製程) 
世芯-KY 成立於 2003 年，專精於提供高複雜度 ASIC 和 SoC
設計 (尤其是後段設計 )與生產服務解決方案。公司多年來深耕
IC 設計服務領域，團隊主要延攬來自於美國矽谷和日本的 IC
設計專家。 世芯- KY 總部位於台北，並於新竹設有 ASIC 製造
中心。基於全球佈局策略，公司在中國、日本和美國設有分
部。

### Page 1

十一月 10, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  1

法說重點摘要

儒鴻 
(1476 TT, NT$348.5, 增加持股)

重點摘要  
1. 目前訂單能見度 保持 6 個月水準；預計 4Q26 旺季營收季增 。明年
營收及毛利率展望初步審慎樂觀看待。  
2. 美國期中選舉後，短期尚未看到明顯追加訂單；但中期而言，隨著
關稅壁 壘政策趨穩與通膨控制政策可能推出，不確定性因素下降。
管理層預 期，品牌商最快 1Q27 才會作出訂單因應變化。  
3. 當前產能 利用率 達滿載。印尼 三期擴廠如計畫進行 。預計 2026 年
資本支出 6-8 億美元， 2027 年進一步增加至 10-12 美元。 
結論 
憑藉著新客戶加入與新產品採用增加，儒鴻營收及獲利成長動能有望
延伸至 2027 年。目前我們給予 「增加持股 」評等及目標價 447 元。 
3Q26 財報 
 3Q26 稅後淨利 17.4 億元，或 每股盈餘 6.36 元，年增 6%、季增
12%，符合預期 。 
 3Q26 營收年增 10%、季增 5%至 105.7 億元， 動能來自 成衣銷售
年增 16%，抵銷布料 1%衰退。 
 受惠新產品與新客戶 帶動 平均銷售單價 提升 ，及匯率有利， 3Q26
毛利率 年增 2.5、季增 1.2 個百分點至 31.2% ；然而， 員工季獎金
增加推升費用率 ，營利率 20.7% ，年增收斂至 1.6 個百分點 。

營運展望更新 
 目前訂單能見度 保持 6 個月水準 ；預計 4Q26 旺季營收季增。  
 2027 年營收及毛利率 展望初步審慎樂觀看待 ，細節將於 11 月底至
12 月初經內部確認後提供 。 
 美國期中選舉後， 短期尚未看到明顯追加訂單；但中期而言， 隨著
關稅壁壘政策趨穩 與通膨控 制政策可能推出 ，不確定性因素 下降。
管理層預 期，品牌商最快 1Q27 才會作出訂單因應變化。 
 新客戶 銷售佔比近月來從 10% 提升至 約 11%，反映消費者 對創新
產品需求 熱絡。此外， 當前仍有數個潛在新客戶洽談中， 部分案件
成功機率較高。  
 前幾大客戶營收佔比無重大變化。 品牌商仍著重於新品開發，走在
市場前端；通路商則尚在觀望期中選舉後 消費者動向。 
 公司採購策略傾向與長期供應商配合，以降低原料成本波動影響。  
 當前產能利用率 達滿載。印尼 三期擴廠如計畫進行 。

重要免責聲明，詳見最終頁

### Page 2

台灣 儒鴻

十一月 10, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  2

 印尼產品組合與越南差距已較過往縮小；但整體而言，越南對複雜
產品線掌握度還是較高，工人技術較成熟。  
 印尼物流時間較長，不確定性因素也較多。  
 預計 2026 年資本支出 6-8 億美元 (對比 1Q-3Q26 約 4-5 億美元)，
並於 2027 年進一步增加至 10-12 美元，主要用於印尼新廠擴建 ，
其次為 越南及柬埔寨廠產線調整與自動化導入 。 
 目前協力廠商供貨佔比 27-28%。 
 成衣產業庫存在過去一段時間緩慢減少，而零售價格呈走升趨勢。  
 隨著美國關稅退款給品牌商 ，品牌也可能討論並制定進一步退還給
供應商之方案。  
 股利政策 將延續過往水準  (過去五年配息率約 65-75%) 。

### Page 3

台灣 儒鴻

十一月 10, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  3

[台湾]～世界的な AI・半導体関連需要の旺盛さがけん引役となり、９月の輸出受注額は過去最高を更新～ 
20 日に発表された９月の輸出受注額は前年同月比＋62.4％となり、 前月 （同＋71.4％） から伸び
が鈍化した。 しかし、 前月比 （季調済） は＋1.1％と前月 （同＋5.9％） から伸びが鈍化したものの、
拡大基調を維持しており、単月ベースの輸出受注額は過去最高を更新している。品目別では、光学
機器関連の受注に下押し圧力が掛かっているものの、 半導体をはじめとする電子部品関連の受注が
引き続き旺盛な動きをみせているほか、情報通信関連、電気製品関連、化学製品関連などでも拡大
の動きが続いている。国・地域別でも、最大の受注先である米国向けが大きく拡大しているほか、
中国本土・ 香港向けや欧州向けも堅調に推移し、ASEAN（東南アジア諸国連合） 向けなど新興国向け

### Page 2

2/4 
も拡大しており、 世界的なＡＩ （人工知能） ・ 半導体関連需要の旺盛さが輸出受注を押し上げている
様子がうかがえる。 
23 日に発表された９月の失業率（季調済）は3.33％となり、前月（3.32％）から0.01pt 悪化し
た。失業者数は前月比＋0.1 万人と前月（同▲0.1 万人）から２ヵ月ぶりの増加に転じている。既
卒者、新卒者問わず全般的に増加基調に転じているほか、自発的失業、非自発的失業ともに増加す
る動きが確認されている。一方の雇用者数は前月比▲0.4 万人と前月（同＋0.2 万人）から２ヵ月
ぶりの減少に転じており、 世界的なＡＩ・半導体需要の旺盛さを反映して製造業で持ち直しの兆し
がみられるほか、建設業では堅調さがうかがえるものの、サービス業で頭打ち感が出ている。雇用
環境に変化の兆しが出ていることを受けて、 労働力人口は前月比▲0.2 万人と前月 （同＋0.1 万人）
から２ヵ月ぶりの減少に転じており、労働市場には一部で弱含みの動きがうかがえる。

[ニュージーランド]～主力の乳製品や食肉を中心に輸出は堅調も、輸入は頭打ち感が強まる～ 
19 日に発表された９月の輸出額は前年同月比＋12.3％となり、 前月 （同＋12.4％） からわずかに
伸びが鈍化した。 しかし、 前月比は＋0.6％と前月 （同＋5.2％） から増加ペースが鈍化したものの、
２ヵ月連続で増加するなど持ち直しの動きが続いている。 財別では、 原油価格の調整を反映して原
油の輸出額が大きく下振れしたほか、 機械製品関連や果物の輸出額も下振れしたものの、 主力の輸
出財である乳製品や食肉の輸出が堅調に推移していることが輸出全体を下支えしている。 国・地域
別では、米国向けやＥＵ向け、日本向けなどが軒並み低調となる一方、最大の輸出先である中国向
けやＡＳＥＡＮ諸国向けが堅調に推移しているほか、 隣国オーストラリア向けにも底堅さがうかが
える。一方、輸入額は前年同月比＋17.7％となり、前月（同＋13.2％）から伸びが加速した。ただ
し、前月比は▲0.2％と前月（同▲7.9％）に続いて２ヵ月連続で減少しており、頭打ち感が強まっ
ている。財別では、原油・石油製品関連の輸入が堅調に推移する一方、機械製品関連のほか、プラ
スチック関連などの素材・部材関連の輸入が下振れしたことが輸入全体の重しとなっている。 その
結果、貿易収支は▲19.71 億ＮＺドルと前月（▲15.36 億ＮＺドル）から赤字幅が拡大した。

[マレーシア]～世界的な AI・半導体関連需要の旺盛さを追い風に、９月の輸出入額はともに過去最高に～ 
19 日に発表された９月の消費者物価は前年同月比＋1.8％となり、 前月 （同＋1.9％） からわずか
に伸びが鈍化した。前月比も＋0.07％と前月（同＋0.29％）から上昇ペースが鈍化しており、政府
による電気料金の負担軽減措置などを背景に住居・光熱費関連の物価上昇率が鈍化したほか、 食料
品価格の上昇の動きも一服しており、 生活必需品を中心にインフレ圧力が後退したことが影響して
いる。 なお、 価格変動の大きい生鮮食品や政府の価格管理対象品目などを除いたコアインフレ率は
前年同月比＋1.7％となり、 前月 （同＋1.7％） から２ヵ月連続で横ばいとなった。 前月比は＋0.29％
と前月（同＋0.29％）と同じペースで上昇しており、基調的な物価上昇圧力は根強く残っている。
一方、 輸送関連の物価上昇率が加速するなど、 エネルギー価格の上昇による影響が一部で顕在化し
ているほか、外食・宿泊関連の物価上昇率も加速しており、物価の動きには品目ごとにばらつきが
生じている。 
同日に発表された９月の輸出額は前年同月比＋54.5％となり、前月（同＋45.5％）から伸びが加
速した。 前月比 （季節調整値） も＋10.0％と前月 （同＋1.3％） から上昇ペースが加速して３ヵ月連
続で拡大しており、 単月ベースの輸出額は過去最高を更新するなど堅調な動きをみせている。 財別
では、 世界的なＡＩ （人工知能） ・ 半導体関連需要の旺盛さを反映して、 半導体など電子部品関連の
輸出が大幅に押し上げられたほか、 原油や天然ガスなどの価格上昇を受けて、 鉱物資源関連の輸出
額も拡大した。一方、パーム油をはじめとする農産品関連の輸出は減少したほか、化学製品関連の
輸出も弱含むなど、 財ごとの動きにはばらつきがみられる。 一方の輸入額は前年同月比＋52.7％と
なり、 前月 （同＋41.1％） から伸びが加速した。 前月比 （季節調整値） も＋11.9％と前月 （同＋1.2％）
から上昇ペースが加速して４ヵ月連続で拡大しており、 単月ベースの輸入額も過去最高を更新した。
輸出の堅調さを反映して、 生産活動に必要な機械製品関連の輸入が大幅に押し上げられたほか、 化
学製品関連などの素材・部材の需要も旺盛に推移している。さらに、原油価格などの上昇を受けて
鉱物資源関連の輸入額も押し上げられている。 その結果、 貿易収支は＋337.24 億リンギとなり、 前
月（＋280.85 億リンギ）から黒字幅が拡大した。

[シンガポール]～生活必需品やサービス物価に上昇圧力は残るも、インフレ率・コアインフレ率は鈍化～ 
23 日に発表された９月の消費者物価は前年同月比＋2.2％となり、 前月 （同＋2.3％） からわずか
に伸びが鈍化した。前月比も＋0.21％と前月（同＋0.62％）から上昇ペースが鈍化したものの、生
鮮品をはじめとする食料品価格は上昇しているほか、電気・ガス価格も高水準で推移するなど、生
活必需品では物価上昇圧力が根強く残る動きがみられる。 住居費と民間輸送費を除いたコアインフ
レ率も前年同月比＋2.0％となり、前月（同＋2.2％）から伸びが鈍化した。前月比も＋0.06％と前
月（同＋0.27％）から上昇ペースが鈍化しており、エネルギー価格の高止まりを反映して輸送関連
サービスの価格に押し上げ圧力がかかる一方、 金融市場におけるＳＧドルの名目実効為替レートの
上昇が輸入物価の上昇圧力を和らげる方向に作用していると考えられる。 さらに、 家具や衣料品な
どの小売関連を中心に財価格の上昇圧力が和らいでいるほか、 サービス物価も上昇ペースが鈍化し
ている。一方で、食料品や一部のサービス価格には上昇圧力が根強く残っており、物価の動きにば
らつきが生じている。
